  - claim after: What God looks to is set against a religious act in the humility passages (the fast, the walk, the house, the broken heart), as in the other passages gathered in 13.1; the offering follows the broken heart
- **1-4** telling added · 10.1 *(opening)* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **The spirit** is troubled, crushed, faint, provoked, and hot or cool in temper. "My spirit faints within me" (Psalm 143:4). "He who has a cool spirit is a man of understanding" (Proverbs 17:27).
  - note: in part: spirit: crushed
- **C-189** telling added · 10.2 *(opening)* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Hearing is the main way in.** The ear receives, and the heart keeps it or refuses it. "All my words that I shall speak to you receive in your heart, and hear with your ears" (Ezekiel 3:10). "Making your ear attentive to wisdom and inclining your heart to understanding" (Proverbs 2:2). When the heart is hard, hearing stops: "Pharaoh's heart was hardened, and he would not listen" (Exodus 7:13). "They made their hearts diamond-hard lest they should hear the law" (Zechariah 7:12). A crushed spirit can block hearing as well. Israel "did not listen to Moses, because of their broken spirit and harsh slavery" (Exodus 6:9).
  - note: in part: a crushed spirit can block hearing
- **C-190** telling added · 10.7 *Being chosen* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **What the chosen are called to.** Being chosen is given as the ground of commands: - **Be strong and do it.** *"the Lord has chosen you to build a house for the sanctuary; be strong and do it"* (1 Chronicles 28:10), with *"serve him with a whole heart and with a willing mind, for the Lord searches all hearts"* (28:9). - **Put on.** *"Put on then, as God's chosen ones, holy and beloved, compassionate hearts, kindness, humility, meekness, and patience"* (Colossians 3:12). What is put on is turned toward others: *"as the Lord has forgiven you, so you also must forgive"* (3:13); *"above all these put on love"* (3:14). - **Stand firm.** *"stand firm and hold to the traditions that you were taught by us"* (2 Thessalonians 2:15). - **Confirm it.** *"be all the more diligent to confirm your calling and election, for if you practice these qualities you will never fall"* (2 Peter 1:10). The qualities are added one to another, from faith to love (1:5–7). The effort follows what was given first: *"His divine power has granted to us all things that pertain to life and godliness"* (1:3). - **Remember.** *"Remember the wondrous works that he has done … children of Jacob, his chosen ones!"* (Psalm 105:5–6); *"Remember these things, O Jacob … you will not be forgotten by me"* (Isaiah 44:21).
  - note: in part: Put on: humility put on, turned toward others
- **C-176** telling added · 10.7 *Others around a choice* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **An inner act weighed by what God chooses.** In one passage what the others offer from within is set against God's own choosing. The people fast and complain, *"Why have we humbled ourselves, and you take no knowledge of it?"*, while *"in the day of your fast you seek your own pleasure, and oppress all your workers"* (Isaiah 58:3). God answers, *"Is such the fast that I choose, a day for a person to humble himself?"* (58:5). What he chooses is turned outward: *"to let the oppressed go free"*, *"to share your bread with the hungry"*, *"not to hide yourself from your own flesh"* (58:6–7). The humbling stood beside oppression in the same people, and it was not what he chooses. The same people *"delight to know my ways"* (58:2), in the same delight word as Isaiah 66:4 in *Whose measure*.
  - note: in part: the humbling beside oppression
- **C-171** telling added · 10.7 *Willing* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **The will made absolute.** Of Nebuchadnezzar: *"Whom he would, he killed, and whom he would, he kept alive; whom he would, he raised up, and whom he would, he humbled"* (Daniel 5:19). The next verse says what happened inside him: *"But when his heart was lifted up and his spirit was hardened so that he dealt proudly, he was brought down"* (5:20). He had been offered counsel (4:27). He was driven out *"until he knew that the Most High God rules the kingdom of mankind and sets over it whom he will"* (5:21). His reason returned when he lifted his eyes to heaven (4:34). His son knew all this, and Daniel says to him, *"you his son, Belshazzar, have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this"* (5:22). Knowing did not move his will.
  - note: in part: Belshazzar has not humbled his heart
- **2-2** telling added · 10.8 *(opening)* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: When Scripture judges a person, it often names the heart. The heart is upright or crooked, pure or deceitful, whole or double, proud or humble. God "weighs the heart" (Proverbs 21:2). The soul is the one who answers for what is done: "the soul who sins shall die". The spirit is judged too: haughty or lowly, crushed or contrite. Chapter 12 takes this further.
  - note: in part: the spirit judged: haughty or lowly, crushed or contrite
- **C-192** telling added · 10.10 *(opening)* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **The spirit is the manner of approach.** "Shall I come to you with a rod, or with love in a spirit of gentleness?" (1 Corinthians 4:21).
  - note: in part: a spirit of gentleness
- **C-194** telling added · 10.10 *Holding authority over another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Turned round.** When "A dispute also arose among them, as to which of them was to be regarded as the greatest", Jesus said, "The kings of the Gentiles exercise lordship over them, and those in authority over them are called benefactors. But not so with you" (Luke 22:24–26). Those who lord it over others take a good name for it as well. In its place: "I am among you as the one who serves" (22:27). Elders are to shepherd "not under compulsion, but willingly", "not domineering over those in your charge, but being examples to the flock" (1 Peter 5:2–3), and all of them, elders too, are to clothe themselves "with humility toward one another" (5:5). Paul had authority and would not use it over faith: "Not that we lord it over your faith, but we work with you for your joy, for you stand firm in your faith" (2 Corinthians 1:24). He had stayed away "to spare you" (1:23).
  - note: in part: elders not domineering; humility toward one another
- **C-194** telling added · 10.10 *Holding authority over another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Under it.** The verses ask something of the one beneath, and give its ground. "Remind them to be submissive to rulers and authorities, to be obedient, to be ready for every good work, to speak evil of no one, to avoid quarreling, to be gentle", and then "For we ourselves were once foolish, disobedient, led astray" (Titus 3:1–3). The ground is remembering what one was, and God's kindness (3:4; 10.3). Under a foreign rule a people can be turned against its own. "Do you not know that the Philistines are rulers over us? What then is this that you have done to us?", said three thousand men of Judah to Samson, and they bound him "that we may give you into the hands of the Philistines" (Judges 15:11–12).
  - note: in part: submission with its ground
- **C-192** telling added · 10.10 *Holding authority over another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Under it.** The verses ask something of the one beneath, and give its ground. "Remind them to be submissive to rulers and authorities, to be obedient, to be ready for every good work, to speak evil of no one, to avoid quarreling, to be gentle", and then "For we ourselves were once foolish, disobedient, led astray" (Titus 3:1–3). The ground is remembering what one was, and God's kindness (3:4; 10.3). Under a foreign rule a people can be turned against its own. "Do you not know that the Philistines are rulers over us? What then is this that you have done to us?", said three thousand men of Judah to Samson, and they bound him "that we may give you into the hands of the Philistines" (Judges 15:11–12).
  - note: in part: remembering what one was
- **C-171** telling added · 10.13 *The heart that turns, and the heart that does not* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Josiah heard the book read and tore his clothes. The answer named his heart: "because your heart was penitent, and you humbled yourself before the Lord … I also have heard you" (2 Kings 22:19). Hezekiah's "heart was proud", and wrath came. "But Hezekiah humbled himself for the pride of his heart … so that the wrath of the Lord did not come upon them in the days of Hezekiah" (2 Chronicles 32:25–26). The same heart provoked the anger and turned it.
  - note: in part: the same heart provoked the anger and turned it
- **C-175** telling added · 10.13 *The heart that turns, and the heart that does not* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Turning can be partial. Rehoboam humbled himself and was spared, "and he did evil, for he did not set his heart to seek the Lord" (2 Chronicles 12:14).
  - note: in part: Rehoboam
- **C-174** telling added · 10.13 *The heart that turns, and the heart that does not* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Distress turns people both ways. Under the judges, "the Lord was moved to pity by their groaning" (Judges 2:18). Manasseh, "when he was in distress, he entreated the favor of the Lord his God and humbled himself greatly" (2 Chronicles 33:12). Ahaz, in the same distress, became "yet more faithless" (28:22). The texts do not say why one turns and the other does not. They set the cases side by side.
  - note: in full
- **C-183** telling added · 10.13 *Shelter, and who can stand* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Zephaniah calls people to seek before the day comes: "Seek the Lord, all you humble of the land, who do his just commands; seek righteousness; seek humility; perhaps you may be hidden on the day of the anger of the Lord" (Zephaniah 2:3). Those called are already humble and already doing his commands, and they are told to seek humility. The verse promises nothing beyond "perhaps", and the hiding is God's to give. What a person has leaned on is tested on that day: "Riches do not profit in the day of wrath, but righteousness delivers from death" (Proverbs 11:4).
  - note: in part: the humble told to seek humility
- **C-204** telling added · 10.13 *Shelter, and who can stand* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Psalm 76 asks the same question and gives the anger's purpose: "But you, you are to be feared! Who can stand before you when once your anger is roused? From the heavens you uttered judgment; the earth feared and was still, when God arose to establish judgment, to save all the humble of the earth" (Psalm 76:7–9). The anger is roused to save the humble, and the fear is the earth falling still before it (10.12).
  - note: in full
- **C-174** telling added · 10.13 *The responses, gathered* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Distress under the anger goes two ways, and the verses do not say why. Ahaz "in the time of his distress … became yet more faithless to the Lord" (2 Chronicles 28:22). Manasseh, "when he was in distress, he entreated the favor of the Lord his God and humbled himself greatly" (2 Chronicles 33:12).
  - note: in part
- **C-171** telling added · 04 *The heart and anger* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Under God's anger, the verses give the heart several roles, each in its own verses. - **The heart that provokes it.** "And the Lord was angry with Solomon, because his heart had turned away from the Lord" (1 Kings 11:9). "Do not harden your hearts, as at Meribah" (Psalm 95:8). "They made their hearts diamond-hard lest they should hear the law … Therefore great anger came from the Lord of hosts" (Zechariah 7:12). "Because of your hard and impenitent heart you are storing up wrath for yourself on the day of wrath" (Romans 2:5). - **The heart that turns it.** "Now it is in my heart to make a covenant with the Lord, the God of Israel, in order that his fierce anger may turn away from us" (2 Chronicles 29:10). "But Hezekiah humbled himself for the pride of his heart … so that the wrath of the Lord did not come upon them" (2 Chronicles 32:26). Josiah was heard "because your heart was penitent" (2 Kings 22:19). - **The heart that is called to be torn, or cut.** "Return to me with all your heart … and rend your hearts and not your garments" (Joel 2:12–13). "Circumcise yourselves to the Lord; remove the foreskin of your hearts … lest my wrath go forth like fire" (Jeremiah 4:4). - **The heart that does not take it in.** "It set him on fire all around, but he did not understand; it burned him up, but he did not take it to heart" (Isaiah 42:25). - **The heart that rejoices** when God's anger acts for it: "You shall see, and your heart shall rejoice … and the hand of the Lord shall be known to his servants, and he shall show his indignation against his enemies" (Isaiah 66:14).
  - note: in part: The heart that turns it
- **C-189** telling added · 04 *The heart is the source* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Chapter 2 starts from that verse. The state of the heart reaches the body: "A tranquil heart gives life to the flesh, but envy makes the bones rot" (Proverbs 14:30). And the heart itself can be revived. God dwells with the lowly "to revive the heart of the contrite" (Isaiah 57:15). "You who seek God, let your hearts revive" (Psalm 69:32). To those who seek him the psalmist says, "May your hearts live forever!" (Psalm 22:26).
  - note: in part: the heart of the contrite revived
- **2-2** telling added · 06 *The human spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: This matters because it is often said that the human spirit never sins. It is true that I found no verse where "the spirit" is the subject of the verb "to sin". But the spirit is certainly judged. It is proud or lowly, faithful or unfaithful, deceitful or clean, and it needs renewing.
  - note: in full
- **5-2** telling added · 06 *The human spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The verbs in these verses are many: the spirit is moved, stirred, fervent ("fervent in spirit", Romans 12:11), willing, crushed, revived and ruled.
  - note: in full
- **2-2** telling added · 06 *The human spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **It is lowly or proud, and it is judged.** "Pride goes before destruction, and a haughty spirit before a fall" (Proverbs 16:18). The next verse chooses the company: "It is better to be of a lowly spirit with the poor than to divide the spoil with the proud" (16:19). "One's pride will bring him low, but he who is lowly in spirit will obtain honor" (Proverbs 29:23). "Blessed are the poor in spirit" (Matthew 5:3). God dwells "with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit" (Isaiah 57:15). God looks to "he who is humble and contrite in spirit and trembles at my word" (Isaiah 66:2). Israel was "a generation whose heart was not steadfast, whose spirit was not faithful to God" (Psalm 78:8). The psalm's blessed man is "the one whose transgression is forgiven" (Psalm 32:1), the man "against whom the Lord counts no iniquity, and in whose spirit there is no deceit" (32:2). The no-deceit stands beside his confession: "I acknowledged my sin to you, and I did not cover my iniquity" (32:5). David prays for "a right spirit" (Psalm 51:10). Paul calls for cleansing "from every defilement of body and spirit" (2 Corinthians 7:1). And "the Lord weighs the spirit" (Proverbs 16:2).
  - note: in part
- **C-189** telling added · 06 *The human spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **It is crushed, and it faints.** "By sorrow of heart the spirit is crushed" (Proverbs 15:13). "A man's spirit will endure sickness, but a crushed spirit who can bear?" (Proverbs 18:14). "My spirit faints within me" (Psalm 143:4). Israel "did not listen to Moses, because of their broken spirit and harsh slavery" (Exodus 6:9). A broken spirit can keep a person from even hearing good news. Job: "My spirit is broken; my days are extinct; the graveyard is ready for me" (Job 17:1).
  - note: in part: a broken spirit can keep a person from hearing
- **C-205** telling added · 11 *3. The parts give way in sequence* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Psalm 143 shows a collapse spreading from one part to the next, then turning. "The enemy has pursued my soul; he has crushed my life to the ground" (143:3). "Therefore my spirit faints within me; my heart within me is appalled" (143:4). Then a turn: "I remember the days of old" (143:5), and "my soul thirsts for you like a parched land" (143:6).
  - note: in full
- **C-205** telling added · 11 *3. The parts give way in sequence* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Proverbs gives the same chain in two lines: "by sorrow of heart the spirit is crushed" (Proverbs 15:13), and "a crushed spirit dries up the bones" (17:22). Heart, then spirit, then body.
  - note: in part
- **C-189** telling added · 11 *4. One word, opposite values* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **Crushing.** Crushed by an enemy, the spirit collapses (Psalm 143:3–4). Offered to God, a crushed spirit is the very sacrifice he wants: "a broken spirit; a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise" (Psalm 51:17).
  - note: in part: crushed by an enemy, or offered to God
- **C-188** telling added · 11 *4. One word, opposite values* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **An offering.** "For you will not delight in sacrifice, or I would give it" (Psalm 51:16). Three verses on: "then will you delight in right sacrifices, in burnt offerings and whole burnt offerings" (51:19). It is the same offering. Between the two stands "a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise" (51:17) (13.1).
  - note: in part
- **C-174** telling added · 11 *What changes the character* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **Distress turns people both ways.** In distress Manasseh "humbled himself greatly" (2 Chronicles 33:12), and Ahaz "became yet more faithless" (28:22). The texts set the cases side by side without saying why. Ease cuts both ways too: "I am exceedingly angry with the nations that are at ease" (Zechariah 1:15).
  - note: in part
- **C-188** telling added · 11 *What changes the character* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **What the one who brings it brings first.** The same offering is refused and then delighted in, and what comes between is a broken heart (Psalm 51:16–19, §4 above). The same weakness is pleaded against and then pleases, and what comes between is God's answer (2 Corinthians 12:8–10).
  - note: in part
- **C-207** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Pride is placed in the heart and in the spirit.
  - note: in full
- **C-208** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Pride is also a false likeness. "You said in your heart, 'I will ascend to heaven … I will make myself like the Most High'" (Isaiah 14:13–14). The prince of Tyre: "you have said, 'I am a god'" (Ezekiel 28:2). The opposite mistake shrinks God to my size: "you thought that I was one like yourself" (Psalm 50:21). Either way, thinking has gone wrong at the point of comparing. Mary sings of what God does with it: "he has scattered the proud in the thoughts of their hearts" (Luke 1:51).
  - note: in full
- **C-209** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Pride can grow from a beauty that was given. Of the great tree: *"I made it beautiful in the mass of its branches, and all the trees of Eden envied it"* (Ezekiel 31:9); *"Because it towered high and set its top among the clouds, and its heart was proud of its height"* (31:10). The others envied the gift, and the tree was proud of it (10.6).
  - note: in part: the great tree
- **C-209** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The king of Tyre is lamented in the same way. *"You were in Eden, the garden of God; every precious stone was your covering"* (Ezekiel 28:13). *"You were blameless in your ways from the day you were created, till unrighteousness was found in you"* (28:15). *"In the abundance of your trade you were filled with violence in your midst, and you sinned"* (28:16). *"Your heart was proud because of your beauty; you corrupted your wisdom for the sake of your splendor"* (28:17). The covering was prepared for him on the day he was made. The heart took the beauty as its ground, and wisdom was spent for splendour. I read the order the verses give: given, blameless, then trade, violence and a proud heart.
  - note: in full
- **C-210** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Death brings pride down. In Isaiah's taunt over the king of Babylon (Isaiah 14:4), the dead greet him: "You too have become as weak as we! You have become like us! Your pomp is brought down to Sheol, the sound of your harps; maggots are laid as a bed beneath you, and worms are your covers" (14:10–11).
  - note: in full
- **C-211** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Pride can design its own honour. The king asks, "What should be done to the man whom the king delights to honor?" And Haman "said to himself, 'Whom would the king delight to honor more than me?'" (Esther 6:6). He spells it out: "let royal robes be brought, which the king has worn, and the horse that the king has ridden, and on whose head a royal crown is set" (6:8). The king answers, "Hurry; take the robes and the horse, as you have said, and do so to Mordecai the Jew" (6:10). Haman leads Mordecai through the square, then "hurried to his house, mourning and with his head covered" (6:12). His wife and wise men tell him, "you will not overcome him but will surely fall before him" (6:13). The act is self-exaltation. The king's delight is only what Haman misreads (10.1, *Delight*). I read that the honour he designed for himself became the measure of his fall.
  - note: in full
- **2-2** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The human spirit is judged here beside the heart. Pride and humility are where the spirit is weighed: haughty or lowly, proud or poor in spirit.
  - note: in part: the spirit weighed: haughty or lowly
- **C-176** telling added · 13.1 *Choosing* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **What he chooses is turned toward others.** "Is not this the fast that I choose: to loose the bonds of wickedness, to undo the straps of the yoke, to let the oppressed go free, and to break every yoke?" (Isaiah 58:6). The self-humbling offered beside oppression is not what he chooses (58:3–5).
  - note: in part: the self-humbling beside oppression
- **C-203** telling added · 13.1 *Willing* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **He does as he wills.** Nebuchadnezzar learned it by being humbled: *"till you know that the Most High rules the kingdom of men and gives it to whom he will"* (Daniel 4:25; 4:17, 32; 5:21). When his reason returned, he said, *"he does according to his will among the host of heaven and among the inhabitants of the earth; and none can stay his hand or say to him, 'What have you done?'"* (4:35). He set it beside *"those who walk in pride he is able to humble"* (4:37).
  - note: in part: those who walk in pride he is able to humble
- **C-188** telling added · 13.1 *Delight* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Not the offering itself.** Five verses say he does not delight in sacrifice, and each sets something else in its place. - "What to me is the multitude of your sacrifices? says the Lord; I have had enough of burnt offerings of rams and the fat of well-fed beasts; I do not delight in the blood of bulls, or of lambs, or of goats" (Isaiah 1:11). Two verses on: "I cannot endure iniquity and solemn assembly" (1:13). - "In sacrifice and offering you have not delighted, but you have given me an open ear" (Psalm 40:6), and the answer: "I delight to do your will, O my God; your law is within my heart" (40:8). - "For you will not delight in sacrifice, or I would give it; you will not be pleased with a burnt offering. The sacrifices of God are a broken spirit; a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise" (Psalm 51:16–17). - "For I desire steadfast love and not sacrifice, the knowledge of God rather than burnt offerings" (Hosea 6:6), after "Your love is like a morning cloud, like the dew that goes early away" (6:4). The "love" of 6:4 and the "steadfast love" of 6:6 are one Hebrew word, *ḥesed*. What he desires is the very thing their love lacks: that it last. - Jesus sends his hearers to learn it: "Go and learn what this means: 'I desire mercy, and not sacrifice.' For I came not to call the righteous, but sinners" (Matthew 9:13); "And if you had known what this means, 'I desire mercy, and not sacrifice,' you would not have condemned the guiltless" (12:7). - And then: "then will you delight in right sacrifices, in burnt offerings and whole burnt offerings" (Psalm 51:19). The offerings he did not delight in (51:16) he will delight in, after the broken heart.
  - note: in part: the offerings delighted in after the broken heart
- **C-206** telling added · 13.1 *Delight* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The verses do not say he has no use for offerings; David says he would give them. Each names something inward that comes first: an ear opened, the law in the heart, a broken heart, a love that lasts. I read that the offering is weighed by what is inside the one who brings it.
  - note: in full
- **C-189** telling added · 14 *Revived, restored, renewed* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **The lowly spirit and the contrite heart revived:** "I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit, to revive the spirit of the lowly, and to revive the heart of the contrite" (Isaiah 57:15). God gives the reason: "I will not contend forever … for the spirit would grow faint before me" (57:16).
  - note: in part

## 2026-10-15 — M09 humility: 10.14 Humility written, angles woven into their chapters, four corrections

**Strand:** humility (M09), fan-out of humbling oneself (#2148)

**Researcher:** "excellent work.  bring the narrative up to date, and ensure that the claims register / narrative is updated" (2026-10-15); placement: "Your recommendation. Ch 14 might expand in scope as we move forward, but it needs its own chapter to encapsulate it properly.  It does have many different  angles that need to be woven onto those chapters in context of each angle."; "humility is not a feeling"; "Jesus is part of divinity and need attention in Ch 13"

**Method:** Humbling oneself fanned out across clusters (218 verses: the M09 words and the humility, meekness, contrite and submission words filed in M05, M10, M23, M24, M53, M73, M75, T2, T3), read in five batches by passage; the 27 no-other-M-code verses read; OT/NT comparison and the role of Jesus; cross-check against the narrative, register, M08 and M47. New 10.14 Humility holds the full account; each angle woven into its chapter in context (10.2, 10.7, 10.9, 10.10, Ch 4, 5, 6, 11, 12, 13.1), pointers in 10.1, 10.12, 10.13, 10.0. Cross-check corrections: Ch 12 "weighed most" (uncounted superlative removed); Mic 6:8 taken out of the anger-at-wrong-to-the-weak statements in 10.8 and 10.13 and placed for what it says; Ch 11 "rest that passes from his heart to ours" restated to the verse; Ch 14 held bullet "seven conditions for the Spirit's work" (2Ch 7:14) removed, the verse now told in 10.14, claim 10-5 withdrawn. Correction 5 (claim 1-1 sources) needs no change: the claim was widened to heart // spirit on first record.

- **C-167** telling added · 10.14 *(opening)* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Humility is not a feeling in the verses. In none that I read is a person said to feel humble. They speak of it in other ways: - **as something a person does.** He humbles himself, before God or before God's word. - **as a standing.** Some are called the humble, the lowly or the meek. - **as something sought and walked in.** "Seek humility" (Zephaniah 2:3). "Walk humbly with your God" (Micah 6:8). - **as a state of the spirit.** A lowly and contrite spirit; a broken heart. - **as a place taken among people.** The lower seat; counting others more significant than oneself. - **as something done to a person.** God humbles to test and to teach, and he brings the proud low. - **as God's own way.** He "looks far down" (Psalm 113:6), and in his Son he "humbled himself" (Philippians 2:8).
  - note: in full
- **C-168** telling added · 10.14 *(opening)* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: In every one of these the verses set humility against a rising: the proud heart, the lifted-up self, the stiff neck, the haughty eyes. I cannot read humility in Scripture without reading what it stands against. Chapter 12 holds pride, and this section holds its counterpart.
  - note: in full
- **C-168** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The counterpart is humbling oneself (10.14). Hezekiah "humbled himself for the pride of his heart" (2 Chronicles 32:26), the verse after "his heart was proud" (32:25). Belshazzar knew what had come of his father's lifted-up heart, and was told, "you … have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this" (Daniel 5:22). The day against pride is named for its end: "The haughty looks of man shall be brought low, and the lofty pride of men shall be humbled, and the Lord alone will be exalted in that day" (Isaiah 2:11).
  - note: pointer
- **C-169** telling added · 10.14 *It faces someone* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Humbling oneself is never done in the air. It is done "before me" (1 Kings 21:29), "before the Lord" (2 Kings 22:19), "before the God of his fathers" (2 Chronicles 33:12), "before your God" (Daniel 10:12). Zedekiah's failure is put this way: "He did not humble himself before Jeremiah the prophet, who spoke from the mouth of the Lord" (2 Chronicles 36:12). To refuse the prophet was to refuse the word he carried.
  - note: in full
- **C-169** telling added · 10.9 *Humbling oneself before God* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: One way a person comes before God is by lowering himself. It is done before him, or before his word. Josiah humbled himself "when you heard how I spoke against this place" (2 Kings 22:19). Zedekiah's failure was that "He did not humble himself before Jeremiah the prophet, who spoke from the mouth of the Lord" (2 Chronicles 36:12). Its words own that God is right and ask for mercy: "The Lord is righteous" (2 Chronicles 12:6); "God, be merciful to me, a sinner!" (Luke 18:13).
  - note: in part: done before him, or before his word
- **C-170** telling added · 10.14 *It answers a word heard* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Most often a person humbles himself when a word reaches him. - Rehoboam and his princes heard "You abandoned me, so I have abandoned you". They "humbled themselves and said, 'The Lord is righteous'" (2 Chronicles 12:5–6). Their humbling was saying that God was right. - Josiah humbled himself "when you heard how I spoke against this place" (2 Kings 22:19). - Ahab, "when Ahab heard those words, … tore his clothes and put sackcloth on his flesh and fasted" (1 Kings 21:27). - Belshazzar had the word in his father's story and did not bow to it: "you … have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this" (Daniel 5:22).
  - note: in full
- **C-170** telling added · 10.9 *Humbling oneself before God* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: One way a person comes before God is by lowering himself. It is done before him, or before his word. Josiah humbled himself "when you heard how I spoke against this place" (2 Kings 22:19). Zedekiah's failure was that "He did not humble himself before Jeremiah the prophet, who spoke from the mouth of the Lord" (2 Chronicles 36:12). Its words own that God is right and ask for mercy: "The Lord is righteous" (2 Chronicles 12:6); "God, be merciful to me, a sinner!" (Luke 18:13).
  - note: in part: its words own that God is right
- **C-171** telling added · 10.14 *Where it happens* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The verses place it in the heart. - "Because your heart was penitent, and you humbled yourself before the Lord" (2 Kings 22:19), or, in the parallel account, "your heart was tender" (2 Chronicles 34:27). - "If then their uncircumcised heart is humbled and they make amends for their iniquity" (Leviticus 26:41), after they "confess their iniquity" (26:40). - "Hezekiah humbled himself for the pride of his heart" (2 Chronicles 32:26). The verse before says, "his heart was proud" (32:25). I read this as the same heart turning back from itself. The pride and the humbling are in one place. - Daniel's is not a response to rebuke at all: "from the first day that you set your heart to understand and humbled yourself before your God, your words have been heard" (Daniel 10:12). Here humbling oneself stands beside setting the heart to understand.
  - note: in full
- **C-171** telling added · 04 *The heart humbled* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The heart is where a person humbles himself. "Because your heart was penitent, and you humbled yourself before the Lord" (2 Kings 22:19); in the parallel account, "your heart was tender" (2 Chronicles 34:27). "If then their uncircumcised heart is humbled" (Leviticus 26:41). Hezekiah "humbled himself for the pride of his heart" (2 Chronicles 32:26), the same heart that was proud a verse before (32:25). Daniel "set your heart to understand and humbled yourself before your God" (Daniel 10:12). Belshazzar's charge is the refusal: "you … have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this" (Daniel 5:22).
  - note: in full
- **C-171** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The counterpart is humbling oneself (10.14). Hezekiah "humbled himself for the pride of his heart" (2 Chronicles 32:26), the verse after "his heart was proud" (32:25). Belshazzar knew what had come of his father's lifted-up heart, and was told, "you … have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this" (Daniel 5:22). The day against pride is named for its end: "The haughty looks of man shall be brought low, and the lofty pride of men shall be humbled, and the Lord alone will be exalted in that day" (Isaiah 2:11).
  - note: in part: Hezekiah and Belshazzar
- **C-172** telling added · 10.14 *What goes with it* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Jesus gives it a face. The tax collector, "standing far off, would not even lift up his eyes to heaven, but beat his breast, saying, 'God, be merciful to me, a sinner!'" The Pharisee beside him prayed, "God, I thank you that I am not like other men … I fast twice a week; I give tithes of all that I get." Jesus: "this man went down to his house justified, rather than the other. For everyone who exalts himself will be humbled, but the one who humbles himself will be exalted" (Luke 18:11–14). The one who lifts himself measures himself against others and lists what he does. The one who humbles himself owns that he is a sinner and asks for mercy.
  - note: in full
- **C-173** telling added · 10.14 *What goes with it* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: James and Peter say it as a run of commands. "Submit yourselves therefore to God. Resist the devil … Draw near to God … Cleanse your hands, you sinners, and purify your hearts, you double-minded. Be wretched and mourn and weep … Humble yourselves before the Lord, and he will exalt you" (James 4:7–10). "Humble yourselves, therefore, under the mighty hand of God so that at the proper time he may exalt you, casting all your anxieties on him, because he cares for you" (1 Peter 5:6–7). In Peter, to humble oneself under God's hand is also to put down what one carries, because he cares. The lifting is "at the proper time". It is his to time.
  - note: in full
- **C-173** telling added · 10.9 *Humbling oneself before God* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: God is said to see it. "When the Lord saw that they humbled themselves" (2 Chronicles 12:7). "Have you seen how Ahab has humbled himself before me?" (1 Kings 21:29). The one who humbles himself puts down what he carries: "Humble yourselves, therefore, under the mighty hand of God so that at the proper time he may exalt you, casting all your anxieties on him, because he cares for you" (1 Peter 5:6–7). James joins it to submitting and drawing near: "Submit yourselves therefore to God … Draw near to God, and he will draw near to you" (James 4:7–8). The whole account is in 10.14.
  - note: in part: puts down what he carries
- **C-174** telling added · 10.14 *Distress does not decide it* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Manasseh, captured and bound, "when he was in distress, … entreated the favor of the Lord his God and humbled himself greatly" (2 Chronicles 33:12). Ahaz, under the same kind of pressure, "In the time of his distress … became yet more faithless to the Lord" (2 Chronicles 28:22). Distress is not what humbles a person. It is met by the heart either way (Chapter 11, *What changes the character*).
  - note: in full
- **C-174** telling added · 10.14 *What it is set against* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: When Hezekiah's couriers carried the call, the cities "laughed them to scorn and mocked them. However, some men of Asher, of Manasseh, and of Zebulun humbled themselves and came to Jerusalem" (2 Chronicles 30:10–11). The same call met two hearts. The verses do not say why.
  - note: in part: the same call met two hearts
- **C-174** telling added · 10.14 *Distress does not decide it* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Nor does it pass from father to son. Amon "did not humble himself before the Lord, as Manasseh his father had humbled himself" (2 Chronicles 33:23).
  - note: in part: Amon
- **C-175** telling added · 10.14 *God sees it, and answers in measure* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The answer is real, and it is measured. - "I will grant them some deliverance … Nevertheless, they shall be servants to him" (2 Chronicles 12:7–8). - "I will not bring the disaster in his days; but in his son's days" (1 Kings 21:29). - The wrath turned "so as not to make a complete destruction" (2 Chronicles 12:12).
  - note: in full
- **C-175** telling added · 10.14 *God sees it, and answers in measure* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: It does not hold by itself. Two verses after Rehoboam humbled himself: "he did evil, for he did not set his heart to seek the Lord" (2 Chronicles 12:14). And in Manasseh, knowing comes after: "Then Manasseh knew that the Lord was God" (2 Chronicles 33:13).
  - note: in part: Rehoboam
- **C-175** telling added · 10.9 *Humbling oneself before God* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: God is said to see it. "When the Lord saw that they humbled themselves" (2 Chronicles 12:7). "Have you seen how Ahab has humbled himself before me?" (1 Kings 21:29). The one who humbles himself puts down what he carries: "Humble yourselves, therefore, under the mighty hand of God so that at the proper time he may exalt you, casting all your anxieties on him, because he cares for you" (1 Peter 5:6–7). James joins it to submitting and drawing near: "Submit yourselves therefore to God … Draw near to God, and he will draw near to you" (James 4:7–8). The whole account is in 10.14.
  - note: in part: God sees it
- **C-176** telling added · 10.14 *A humbling God does not take knowledge of* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Ahab's sackcloth and fasting were seen and answered (1 Kings 21:27–29). Israel's were not. I read the difference in what went with them. Ahab acted when the word came against him. Israel fasted while seeking its own pleasure, and left the naked it saw uncovered.
  - note: in full
- **C-177** telling added · 10.14 *A humbling God does not take knowledge of* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The New Testament names the same thing. The noun for humility appears as "asceticism": "Let no one disqualify you, insisting on asceticism … puffed up without reason by his sensuous mind", and "self-made religion and asceticism and severity to the body, but they are of no value in stopping the indulgence of the flesh" (Colossians 2:18, 23). What looks like lowering puffs the person up. The Pharisee's "I fast twice a week" stands on the same side (Luke 18:12).
  - note: in full
- **C-178** telling added · 10.14 *Being humbled by God* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **To show the heart, and to teach.** "that he might humble you, testing you to know what was in your heart, whether you would keep his commandments or not. And he humbled you and let you hunger and fed you with manna … that he might make you know that man does not live by bread alone" (Deuteronomy 8:2–3). The end is named: "to do you good in the end" (8:16). The danger it guards against comes in the same chapter. When plenty comes, "your heart be lifted up, and you forget the Lord", and you "say in your heart, 'My power and the might of my hand have gotten me this wealth'" (8:14, 17). The next chapter goes on: "Do not say in your heart … 'It is because of my righteousness'" (9:4).
  - note: in full
- **C-178** telling added · 04 *The heart humbled* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: God humbles too, and what he reaches is the heart: "testing you to know what was in your heart" (Deuteronomy 8:2); "he bowed their hearts down with hard labor" (Psalm 107:12). The opposite is named: "Before destruction a man's heart is haughty" (Proverbs 18:12).
  - note: in part: testing the heart; hearts bowed down
- **C-179** telling added · 10.14 *Being humbled by God* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Being humbled can turn a person, or harden him.** "The Lord humbled Judah because of Ahaz", and Ahaz grew "yet more faithless" (2 Chronicles 28:19, 22). Manasseh, brought low, humbled himself (33:12).
  - note: in full
- **C-180** telling added · 10.14 *The proud brought low, the lowly raised* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **What is brought low is a bearing, not only a rank.** - "The haughty looks of man shall be brought low, and the lofty pride of men shall be humbled, and the Lord alone will be exalted in that day" (Isaiah 2:11, 17). - "the eyes of the haughty are brought low" (Isaiah 5:15). - "I will put an end to the pomp of the arrogant, and lay low the pompous pride of the ruthless" (Isaiah 13:11).
  - note: in full
- **C-180** telling added · 10.14 *The proud brought low, the lowly raised* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: In Isaiah the setting is a land "filled with silver and gold … filled with idols; they bow down to the work of their hands" (2:7–8). I read being brought low there as the lifted-up thing made to give way to the One who is high. The verse gives the end: "the Lord alone will be exalted".
  - note: in part: the lifted-up thing made to give way
- **C-180** telling added · 12 *Pride and the lifted-up heart and spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The counterpart is humbling oneself (10.14). Hezekiah "humbled himself for the pride of his heart" (2 Chronicles 32:26), the verse after "his heart was proud" (32:25). Belshazzar knew what had come of his father's lifted-up heart, and was told, "you … have not humbled your heart, though you knew all this" (Daniel 5:22). The day against pride is named for its end: "The haughty looks of man shall be brought low, and the lofty pride of men shall be humbled, and the Lord alone will be exalted in that day" (Isaiah 2:11).
  - note: in part: the day against pride
- **C-181** telling added · 10.14 *The proud brought low, the lowly raised* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Only God does it.** Job is challenged: "look on everyone who is proud and abase him … Then will I also acknowledge to you that your own right hand can save you" (Job 40:11–14). He cannot. "Not from the east or from the west … comes lifting up, but it is God who executes judgment, putting down one and lifting up another" (Psalm 75:6–7).
  - note: in full
- **C-182** telling added · 10.14 *The proud brought low, the lowly raised* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Where the boast goes.** "Let the lowly brother boast in his exaltation, and the rich in his humiliation, because like a flower of the grass he will pass away" (James 1:9–10). "God chose what is low and despised in the world … so that no human being might boast in the presence of God … 'Let the one who boasts, boast in the Lord'" (1 Corinthians 1:28–31). I read both as moving the boast from what a person has to what God does. The rich man may boast in being brought low, because it tells him the truth about himself.
  - note: in full
- **C-183** telling added · 10.14 *The humble* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **They seek.** "Seek the Lord, all you humble of the land, who do his just commands; seek righteousness; seek humility; perhaps you may be hidden on the day of the anger of the Lord" (Zephaniah 2:3). Those already called humble are told to seek humility. I read from this that humility is not held once for all. Of the remnant God leaves: "a people humble and lowly. They shall seek refuge in the name of the Lord … they shall do no injustice and speak no lies" (Zephaniah 3:12–13).
  - note: in full
- **C-184** telling added · 10.14 *The humble* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **They are glad at what God did for another.** "My soul makes its boast in the Lord; let the humble hear and be glad" (Psalm 34:2). "When the humble see it they will be glad; you who seek God, let your hearts revive" (Psalm 69:32). In both psalms one man's rescue is the gladness of others.
  - note: in full
- **C-185** telling added · 10.14 *The humble* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **They are taught.** "He leads the humble in what is right, and teaches the humble his way" (Psalm 25:9). The man praying it says, "Remember not the sins of my youth" and "pardon my guilt, for it is great" (25:7, 11). I read the humble here as the teachable, and the teachable as those who own their guilt.
  - note: in full
- **C-186** telling added · 10.14 *The humble* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **The meek, where another provokes.** "Refrain from anger, and forsake wrath! Fret not yourself; it tends only to evil … But the meek shall inherit the land and delight themselves in abundant peace" (Psalm 37:8–11). "Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth" (Matthew 5:5). Moses was "very meek, more than all people who were on the face of the earth" (Numbers 12:3). That is said at the moment Miriam and Aaron spoke against him, and it is the Lord, not Moses, who answers them. I read meekness, in both places, as standing where another provokes, and leaving the answer to God.
  - note: in full
- **C-187** telling added · 10.14 *Humility as a way of walking* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **With hearing.** In both places where "humility comes before honor", the verse beside it is about hearing. - "The ear that listens to life-giving reproof will dwell among the wise. Whoever ignores instruction despises himself, but he who listens to reproof gains intelligence" (Proverbs 15:31–32). - "If one gives an answer before he hears, it is his folly and shame" (Proverbs 18:13). - James says it again: "let every person be quick to hear, slow to speak, slow to anger … receive with meekness the implanted word, which is able to save your souls" (James 1:19–21). - God looks to "he who is humble and contrite in spirit and trembles at my word" (Isaiah 66:2).
  - note: in full
- **C-187** telling added · 10.14 *Humility as a way of walking* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: I read humility, in these verses, as the ear that can take a reproof and a word, and does not answer before it hears (10.2).
  - note: in part
- **C-187** telling added · 10.2 *Humility and hearing* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Twice Proverbs says "humility comes before honor", and both times the verse beside it is about the ear. "The ear that listens to life-giving reproof will dwell among the wise. Whoever ignores instruction despises himself, but he who listens to reproof gains intelligence" (Proverbs 15:31–32). "If one gives an answer before he hears, it is his folly and shame" (Proverbs 18:13). James puts them in one sentence: "let every person be quick to hear, slow to speak, slow to anger … receive with meekness the implanted word" (James 1:19–21). And God looks to the one who "trembles at my word" (Isaiah 66:2). I read humility here as the ear that can take a word against itself, and does not answer before it hears. The whole account is in 10.14.
  - note: in full
- **C-188** telling added · 10.14 *Humility as a way of walking* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Instead of the offering.** Three more passages set what God looks to against a religious act: - the fast (Isaiah 58) - the house and the sacrifices: "But this is the one to whom I will look: he who is humble and contrite in spirit" (Isaiah 66:1–3) - "For you will not delight in sacrifice … The sacrifices of God are a broken spirit; a broken and contrite heart, O God, you will not despise" (Psalm 51:16–17)
  - note: in full
- **C-188** telling added · 10.14 *Humility as a way of walking* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Walking with God.** "With what shall I come before the Lord … Shall I come before him with burnt offerings …? He has told you, O man, what is good; and what does the Lord require of you but to do justice, and to love kindness, and to walk humbly with your God?" (Micah 6:6–8). The question is what to bring, and the answer is a way to walk. "Humbly" is "with your God": a walk in his company, not a gift handed over.
  - note: in part: what to bring, and a way to walk
- **C-188** telling added · 10.14 *Humility as a way of walking* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The psalm goes on, "then will you delight in right sacrifices" (51:19). The offering is not done away with. It follows the broken heart.
  - note: in part
- **C-189** telling added · 10.14 *A lowly and contrite spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Where God and a broken heart meet in these verses, he is near and he heals: - "The Lord is near to the brokenhearted and saves the crushed in spirit" (Psalm 34:18) - "He heals the brokenhearted and binds up their wounds" (Psalm 147:3) - the anointed one is sent "to bind up the brokenhearted" (Isaiah 61:1)
  - note: in full
- **C-189** telling added · 10.14 *A lowly and contrite spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **The breaking has more than one cause.** - **Over one's own sin**, and brought to God: Psalm 51, and "I am feeble and crushed; I groan because of the tumult of my heart" because "my iniquities have gone over my head", with "all my longing is before you" (Psalm 38:4, 8–9). - **By what others do:** "Reproaches have broken my heart, so that I am in despair. I looked for pity, but there was none" (Psalm 69:20). "For I am poor and needy, and my heart is stricken within me" (Psalm 109:22). - **By slavery:** "they did not listen to Moses, because of their broken spirit and harsh slavery" (Exodus 6:9). That broken spirit could not hear the promise of rescue. - **By God's word against what is seen:** "My heart is broken within me … because of the Lord and because of his holy words" (Jeremiah 23:9). - **By sorrow:** "by sorrow of heart the spirit is crushed" (Proverbs 15:13), and "a crushed spirit who can bear?" (Proverbs 18:14).
  - note: in full
- **C-189** telling added · 10.14 *A lowly and contrite spirit* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The verses do not divide contrition from despair by name. They give the cause of the breaking, and whether the broken heart is brought to God. Where it is, God is near, heals and revives. The spirit's own part is in Chapter 6.
  - note: in full
- **C-189** telling added · 04 *The heart humbled* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The heart can be broken. "Reproaches have broken my heart, so that I am in despair" (Psalm 69:20). "I am poor and needy, and my heart is stricken within me" (Psalm 109:22). "I groan because of the tumult of my heart" (Psalm 38:8). The prophet: "My heart is broken within me … because of the Lord and because of his holy words" (Jeremiah 23:9). And "He heals the brokenhearted and binds up their wounds" (Psalm 147:3).
  - note: in part
- **C-190** telling added · 10.14 *Toward one another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Counting others.** "Do nothing from selfish ambition or conceit, but in humility count others more significant than yourselves. Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others" (Philippians 2:3–4). "Do not be haughty, but associate with the lowly. Never be wise in your own sight" (Romans 12:16).
  - note: in full
- **C-190** telling added · 10.14 *Toward one another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Worn.** "Put on then … compassionate hearts, kindness, humility, meekness, and patience, bearing with one another and … forgiving each other" (Colossians 3:12–13). "Clothe yourselves, all of you, with humility toward one another" (1 Peter 5:5). It is always named with patience, bearing with, forgiving, sympathy and a tender heart (Ephesians 4:2; 1 Peter 3:8). It keeps "the unity of the Spirit in the bond of peace" (Ephesians 4:3).
  - note: in full
- **C-190** telling added · 10.10 *Humility toward one another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Between people, humility is a way of counting the other: "in humility count others more significant than yourselves. Let each of you look not only to his own interests, but also to the interests of others" (Philippians 2:3–4). It is set against "selfish ambition or conceit" (2:3), and against being "haughty" or "wise in your own sight" (Romans 12:16). It is worn toward others: "Clothe yourselves, all of you, with humility toward one another" (1 Peter 5:5). And it is named beside "bearing with one another in love" (Ephesians 4:2).
  - note: in full
- **C-191** telling added · 10.14 *Toward one another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **The seat and the title.** "do not sit down in a place of honor … go and sit in the lowest place, so that when your host comes he may say to you, 'Friend, move up higher'" (Luke 14:8–10). The proverb had said it: "Do not put yourself forward in the king's presence … it is better to be told, 'Come up here,' than to be put lower in the presence of a noble" (Proverbs 25:6–7). To the question of titles: "you are not to be called rabbi … The greatest among you shall be your servant" (Matthew 23:8, 11). And: "Whoever humbles himself like this child is the greatest in the kingdom of heaven" (Matthew 18:4). The lower place is taken before anyone gives it, and the honour is given, not taken.
  - note: in full
- **C-191** telling added · 10.7 *Taking the lower place* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Some choices are choices of place. Jesus told a parable "when he noticed how they chose the places of honor": "do not sit down in a place of honor … But when you are invited, go and sit in the lowest place, so that when your host comes he may say to you, 'Friend, move up higher'" (Luke 14:7–10). The proverb had the same counsel: "Do not put yourself forward in the king's presence … for it is better to be told, 'Come up here,' than to be put lower in the presence of a noble" (Proverbs 25:6–7). The lower place is chosen before anyone else must give it, and the honour, if it comes, is given.
  - note: in full
- **C-192** telling added · 10.14 *Toward one another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **In meeting someone who is wrong.** Gentleness shows in how one meets a wrong: - "restore him in a spirit of gentleness. Keep watch on yourself, lest you too be tempted" (Galatians 6:1); "if anyone thinks he is something, when he is nothing, he deceives himself" (6:3) - "correcting his opponents with gentleness. God may perhaps grant them repentance" (2 Timothy 2:25) - "make a defense … with gentleness and respect" (1 Peter 3:15) - "to be gentle, and to show perfect courtesy toward all people. For we ourselves were once foolish" (Titus 3:2–3)
  - note: in full
- **C-192** telling added · 10.14 *Toward one another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: What these verses join to gentleness is watching oneself, remembering what one was, and leaving the outcome to God.
  - note: in full
- **C-192** telling added · 10.10 *Humility toward one another* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: It shows in how a wrong is met: "restore him in a spirit of gentleness. Keep watch on yourself, lest you too be tempted" (Galatians 6:1); "correcting his opponents with gentleness" (2 Timothy 2:25). The reason given is one's own past: "For we ourselves were once foolish" (Titus 3:3).
  - note: in full
- **C-193** telling added · 10.14 *Submitting* (recorded, 2026-10-15)

- **C-200** telling added · 10.14 *The Old Testament and the New* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: Everything above is in both Testaments: - humbling oneself before God, and God's answer - the proud brought low and the humble raised - favour to the humble - the lower seat - the meek inheriting - God humbling for good - a humbling he does not accept - humility beside hearing - the humble king - God lowering himself
  - note: in full
- **C-200** telling added · 10.14 *The Old Testament and the New* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **Before God, then also toward one another.** In the Old Testament humbling is almost always before God. Kings and the people humble themselves, and the answer is wrath turned or disaster delayed. In the New, much of what is said is about humility toward one another. It always has a reason that faces God, and the answer is named as being justified, grace, and rest for the soul.
  - note: in full
- **C-200** telling added · 10.14 *The Old Testament and the New* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **The new thing: Christ.** He is the one who is lowly in heart, who humbled himself to death, and in whose mind the humble live. What God did in looking down, in his gentleness and in his breaking over a heart, the Son did in a human life. What the Old Testament said of man, "you have made him a little lower than the heavenly beings … you have put all things under his feet" (Psalm 8:5–6), the letter to the Hebrews reads as "namely Jesus" (Hebrews 2:6–9).
  - note: in full
- **C-201** telling added · 10.14 *When humility changes character* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The same words wear different faces. - **One Hebrew verb** is a king humbling himself before God, God humbling a people, and an enemy "subdued" in war. When a person humbles himself, the heart is named and God answers. When an enemy is beaten, no inner act is named. - **One Greek noun** is humility toward one another, and a self-made asceticism that puffs up (Colossians 2:18, 23). - **The word "humble" itself** is a charge flung at Paul: "I who am humble when face to face with you, but bold toward you when I am away!" (2 Corinthians 10:1). - **Being brought low** is God's judgment on the proud (Isaiah 2:11), a test for good (Deuteronomy 8:16), a circumstance met with contentment (Philippians 4:12), and the place Christ went (Philippians 2:8). - **Submitting** is right before God, right for the Lord's sake among people, and wrong to those who would enslave the gospel (Galatians 2:5).
  - note: in full
- **C-201** telling added · 11 *What changes the character* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **Who lowers whom, and before whom.** A king humbling himself before God is answered (2 Chronicles 12:7). A people humbled by God may turn or harden (2 Chronicles 33:12; 28:22). A nation humbled by an enemy is only "subdued" (Judges 3:30). The noun for humility toward one another also names a self-made severity that puffs a person up (Colossians 2:18, 23), and submission is right before God and wrong to those who would enslave the gospel (Galatians 2:5) (10.14).
  - note: in full
- **C-202** telling added · 13.1 *Lowering himself* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: I read the lowering that the Old Testament tells of God, looking down, dwelling with the lowly, his gentleness, his being broken over a heart, as done in the Son in a human life.
  - note: in full
- **C-202** telling added · 13.1 *Lowering himself* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: The Lord is high, and the verses keep saying that he looks low. - "Who is like the Lord our God, who is seated on high, who looks far down on the heavens and the earth? He raises the poor from the dust" (Psalm 113:5–7). The verb for looking far down is the one used when he brings the proud low. - "For though the Lord is high, he regards the lowly, but the haughty he knows from afar" (Psalm 138:6). - "I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and lowly spirit, to revive the spirit of the lowly, and to revive the heart of the contrite" (Isaiah 57:15). - David: "You have given me the shield of your salvation, and your gentleness made me great" (2 Samuel 22:36; Psalm 18:35). The word rendered "gentleness" is the word for humility. - Over his people's unfaithfulness: "how I have been broken over their whoring heart that has departed from me" (Ezekiel 6:9).
  - note: in part
- **C-202** telling added · 10.14 *The Old Testament and the New* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: - **The new thing: Christ.** He is the one who is lowly in heart, who humbled himself to death, and in whose mind the humble live. What God did in looking down, in his gentleness and in his breaking over a heart, the Son did in a human life. What the Old Testament said of man, "you have made him a little lower than the heavenly beings … you have put all things under his feet" (Psalm 8:5–6), the letter to the Hebrews reads as "namely Jesus" (Hebrews 2:6–9).
  - note: in part: what God did, the Son did in a human life
- **C-203** telling added · 13.1 *Lowering himself* (recorded, 2026-10-15)
  - passage before: —
  - passage after: **Toward the humble and the proud.** He sees the one who humbles himself and answers: "When the Lord saw that they humbled themselves" (2 Chronicles 12:7); "Have you seen how Ahab has humbled himself before me?" (1 Kings 21:29). He gives favour: "to the humble he gives favor" (Proverbs 3:34); "God opposes the proud but gives grace to the humble" (James 4:6). He humbles to test, "to do you good in the end" (Deuteronomy 8:16). He brings the proud low, "and the Lord alone will be exalted in that day" (Isaiah 2:11). He looks on a humble estate (Luke 1:48). What this means for the one who humbles himself is in 10.14.
  - note: in full

## 2026-10-14 — OT-02 die, dead and perish words: Ch 2 death part rebuilt, claims registered

**Strand:** life and death (M25), OT-02 Q1 What is death?

**Researcher:** "proceed with the weave as proposed, include the perish pull. What I am seeing is encouraging that we heading in right direction." (2026-10-14), on check v2; earlier: "check the completeness and validity of existing claims, check narration for unsubstantiated or unrecorded claims, and check the verses for perspectives that were missed in the first round"; "have you adequately recognised the different perspectives of death related to 'spiritual death', 'dead to sin', 'causes of death'"

**Method:** Die/death words (H4191, H4194, G0599, G2288) checked against the claims and Ch 2 by unit-2 face; the dead/die words hidden in T-codes (G3498 and 16 others, 250 verses) and the perish words (H0006, G0622, G0684, 271 verses) pulled and read. Ch 2 death part rebuilt by kind of death (Death spoken of in more than one way; Death in the living with stated causes and the way out; new Dead to sin; new Those left behind); 10 statements corrected (OT-02.10 counts); 31 + 16 claims registered with tellings. Pointers in 10.10, 10.12 (working note removed), 12 and 14.

- **C-84** telling changed · 02 *What death is* (recorded, 2026-10-14)
  - passage before: The same death is met in different ways. "One dies in his full vigor, being wholly at ease and secure"; "Another dies in bitterness of soul, never having tasted of prosperity" (Job 21:23, 25). Nothing goes with the dead: "For when he dies he will carry nothing away; his glory will not go down after him" (Psalm 49:17); "When the wicked dies, his hope will perish" (Proverbs 11:7). No one knows when it will come: "I am old; I do not know the day of my death" (Genesis 27:2). Yet the Preacher can write that "the day of death" is better "than the day of birth" (Ecclesiastes 7:1).
  - passage after: The same death is met in different ways. "One dies in his full vigor, being wholly at ease and secure"; "Another dies in bitterness of soul, never having tasted of prosperity" (Job 21:23, 25). Nothing goes with the dead: "For when he dies he will carry nothing away; his glory will not go down after him" (Psalm 49:17); "When the wicked dies, his hope will perish" (Proverbs 11:7). No one knows when it will come: "I am old; I do not know the day of my death" (Genesis 27:2). Yet the Preacher can write that "the day of death" is better "than the day of birth" (Ecclesiastes 7:1). Asaph, who says "I was envious" of the wicked, noticed that "they have no pangs until death" (Psalm 73:3–4).
- **C-85** telling changed · 02 *Death and sin* (recorded, 2026-10-14)
  - passage before: Scripture ties death to sin from the first command. "of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat, for in the day that you eat of it you shall surely die" (Genesis 2:17). The first word spoken against that command denies the death: "But the serpent said to the woman, 'You will not surely die.'" (Genesis 3:4).
  - passage after: Scripture ties death to sin from the first command. "of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil you shall not eat, for in the day that you eat of it you shall surely die" (Genesis 2:17). The woman repeats it with more than was said: "neither shall you touch it, lest you die" (Genesis 3:3). The first word spoken against that command denies the death: "But the serpent said to the woman, 'You will not surely die.'" (Genesis 3:4).
- **C-87** telling changed · 02 *Death and sin* (recorded, 2026-10-14)
  - passage before: Each person answers for his own. "the soul who sins shall die" (Ezekiel 18:4). "But each one shall die for his own sin" (2 Kings 14:6). Jesus says it of unbelief: "unless you believe that I am he you will die in your sins" (John 8:24). John speaks of "sin that leads to death" and of sin that does not, and tells a believer to ask life for a brother whose sin does not lead to death (1 John 5:16–17). He does not say which sin leads to death, and I do not guess.
  - passage after: Each person answers for his own. "the soul who sins shall die" (Ezekiel 18:4). "But each one shall die for his own sin" (2 Kings 14:6; from Deuteronomy 24:16). Jeremiah repeats it: "everyone shall die for his own iniquity" (Jeremiah 31:30). Jesus says it of unbelief: "unless you believe that I am he you will die in your sins" (John 8:24). John speaks of "sin that leads to death" and of sin that does not, and tells a believer to ask life for a brother whose sin does not lead to death (1 John 5:16–17). He does not say which sin leads to death, and I do not guess.
- **C-88** telling changed · 02 *Death in the living* (recorded, 2026-10-14)
  - passage before: Scripture also speaks of death inside a living person: - **The heart can die first.** Nabal's "heart died within him, and he became as a stone" ten days before he died (below). - **The living can be called dead.** Paul writes to living people, "you, who were dead in your trespasses and the uncircumcision of your flesh" (Colossians 2:13; also Ephesians 2:5). - **A person can be called alive and be dead.** "You have the reputation of being alive, but you are dead" (Revelation 3:1). "She who is self-indulgent is dead even while she lives" (1 Timothy 5:6). Paul speaks of people "alienated from the life of God because of the ignorance that is in them, due to their hardness of heart" (Ephesians 4:18). - **The mind can be set on it.** "For to set the mind on the flesh is death, but to set the mind on the Spirit is life and peace" (Romans 8:6). - **A person can abide in it.** "Whoever does not love abides in death" (1 John 3:14). And "worldly grief produces death" (2 Corinthians 7:10). - **A person can cry out under it.** "Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from this body of death?" (Romans 7:24). - **A church can be near it.** "strengthen what remains and is about to die" (Revelation 3:2).
  - passage after: Scripture also speaks of death inside a living person: - **The heart can die first.** Nabal's "heart died within him, and he became as a stone" ten days before he died (below). - **The living can be called dead.** Paul writes to living people, "you, who were dead in your trespasses and the uncircumcision of your flesh" (Colossians 2:13; also Ephesians 2:5). - **A person can be called alive and be dead.** "You have the reputation of being alive, but you are dead" (Revelation 3:1). "She who is self-indulgent is dead even while she lives" (1 Timothy 5:6). Paul speaks of people "alienated from the life of God because of the ignorance that is in them, due to their hardness of heart" (Ephesians 4:18). - **The mind can be set on it.** "For to set the mind on the flesh is death, but to set the mind on the Spirit is life and peace" (Romans 8:6). - **A person can abide in it.** "Whoever does not love abides in death" (1 John 3:14). And "worldly grief produces death" (2 Corinthians 7:10). - **A person can cry out under it.** "Wretched man that I am! Who will deliver me from this body of death?" (Romans 7:24). - **A church can be near it.** "strengthen what remains and is about to die" (Revelation 3:2). - **Works and faith can be dead.** "repentance from dead works" (Hebrews 6:1); the blood of Christ will "purify our conscience from dead works to serve the living God" (9:14); "faith by itself, if it does not have works, is dead" (James 2:17). - **It can lie behind a fair outside.** "you are like whitewashed tombs, which outwardly appear beautiful, but within are full of dead people's bones and all uncleanness" (Matthew 23:27). - **A people can die while it lives.** Ephraim "incurred guilt through Baal and died" (Hosea 13:1). False teachers are "twice dead, uprooted" (Jude 12). - **Inner things can perish.** "the hope of the godless shall perish" (Job 8:13). "My endurance has perished; so has my hope from the Lord" (Lamentations 3:18). "Our bones are dried up, and our hope is lost" (Ezekiel 37:11). "Courage shall fail both king and officials" (Jeremiah 4:9); the word behind "courage" is *lēḇ* ("heart"), and the verb is *ʾāḇaḏ* ("perish"). "The wisdom of their wise men shall perish" (Isaiah 29:14). "Truth has perished; it is cut off from their lips" (Jeremiah 7:28). "A bribe corrupts the heart" (Ecclesiastes 7:7), with the same verb.
- **C-89** telling changed · 02 *Sleep and waking* (recorded, 2026-10-14)
  - passage before: Jesus speaks of death as sleep. "the girl is not dead but sleeping. And they laughed at him" (Matthew 9:24). Of Lazarus he says, "Our friend Lazarus has fallen asleep, but I go to awaken him." The disciples hear it as rest: "Lord, if he has fallen asleep, he will recover." So "Jesus told them plainly, 'Lazarus has died'" (John 11:11–14). The picture had to be said plainly before they understood it. At his own death, "many bodies of the saints who had fallen asleep were raised" (Matthew 27:52).
  - passage after: Jesus speaks of death as sleep. "the girl is not dead but sleeping. And they laughed at him" (Matthew 9:24); Luke adds, "knowing that she was dead" (Luke 8:53). Of Lazarus he says, "Our friend Lazarus has fallen asleep, but I go to awaken him." The disciples hear it as rest: "Lord, if he has fallen asleep, he will recover." So "Jesus told them plainly, 'Lazarus has died'" (John 11:11–14). The picture had to be said plainly before they understood it. At his own death, "many bodies of the saints who had fallen asleep were raised" (Matthew 27:52).
